### Cycle 178 (2026-07-24) — closing the biometric-auth coverage gap; a concurrent edit reshapes this cycle's scope mid-pick

Continued the roadmap item #17 sweep. Checked `git status` before picking a target, as always — and this time it mattered directly: `AssetAccessService`, one of the 8 remaining candidates, had active uncommitted changes from the concurrent parallel session (`backend/src/modules/platform-assets/asset-access.service.ts`, a small ongoing diff). Writing a new test file against a service mid-edit by another session risks the two changes colliding or the new tests being written against a version of the code that's about to change out from under them — so it was deliberately skipped this cycle, staying on the roadmap list rather than being picked blind.

Picked the next-most-sensitive pair instead: `BiometricService` + `BiometricController` (`src/modules/auth/services/biometric.service.ts`, `src/modules/auth/biometric.controller.ts`) — real biometric enrollment, challenge/response verification, and account-lockout logic, genuinely security-critical and previously covered by neither a dedicated spec nor any incidental coverage from another test. Read both files in full first.

**`biometric.service.spec.ts` (18 tests).** Followed this codebase's own established pattern for security-service tests (`two-factor.service.spec.ts`'s `Test.createTestingModule` + mocked-repository style) rather than inventing a new one. Deliberately did **not** mock Node's built-in `crypto` module — `hashToken()`'s SHA-256 hashing and `verifyToken()`'s `crypto.timingSafeEqual()` comparison run for real in every test, replicated in the test file via the same `crypto.createHash('sha256')` call the service itself uses, so tests assert against genuinely-computed hashes rather than a stubbed "looks right" value. Covered:
- **Enrollment**: a fresh device creates a new `BiometricConfig` via `create()`+`save()` and returns a plaintext challenge token whose SHA-256 hash matches what's actually stored (confirming the plaintext token is never itself persisted); re-enrolling an already-known device updates in place (no `create()` call) and resets `failedAttempts`/`lockedUntil`, with an unspecified `deviceName` correctly falling back to the existing stored value rather than being overwritten with `undefined`.
- **The lockout state machine**, tested at every transition rather than just the happy path: not-enrolled rejects immediately; a still-locked account rejects without even touching `failedAttempts` (confirming the lockout check short-circuits before the crypto comparison); a lockout whose window has already elapsed correctly allows verification to proceed again; a wrong challenge response below the 5-attempt threshold increments `failedAttempts` and rejects without locking; reaching exactly the 5th failed attempt sets `lockedUntil` to a real future timestamp and rejects with the lockout message; a correct response resets `failedAttempts`/`lockedUntil` to zero/`null`, bumps `usageCount`, stamps `lastUsedAt`, rotates to a fresh single-use challenge token (confirmed different from the one just consumed), and issues real access/refresh JWTs via the mocked `JwtService`.
- **A defensive resilience test, not just a happy-path/failure-path pair**: `crypto.timingSafeEqual()` throws a `RangeError` (not returns `false`) when its two buffers differ in length — genuinely possible if a stored hash were ever corrupted or came from a legacy hashing scheme of a different output length. Constructed exactly that scenario (a stored `challengeToken` of the wrong length) and confirmed the service's outer `catch` converts the raw `RangeError` into a clean `UnauthorizedException('Biometric verification failed')` instead of letting an unrelated internal error type leak to the caller — the same "fail closed with a clear message, not an uncontrolled crash" idiom this whole `strictNullChecks` era of cycles established for Stripe/webhook gaps, here applying to a genuinely different failure class (a runtime crypto exception, not a null/undefined value).
- **The remaining CRUD-shaped methods**: `getBiometricConfig`'s query narrows correctly with/without an optional `deviceId` filter; `disableBiometric` flips `isEnabled` and throws `BadRequestException` when the config doesn't exist; `deleteBiometricConfig` scopes its delete to all of a user's devices or just one; `resetFailedAttempts` issues the correct `update()` call; `getBiometricStats` aggregates `totalUsages`/`lastUsed`/per-device stats correctly, including the empty-state case (zero devices, `lastUsed: null`) rather than crashing on an empty array.

**`biometric.controller.spec.ts` (7 tests).** A thin pass-through layer, tested by direct construction (`new BiometricController(mockService)`, matching this codebase's existing `auth.controller.spec.ts` pattern rather than a full Nest `TestingModule` for a plain-constructor class) with the service mocked. The point of these tests isn't re-testing the service's logic — it's confirming the controller's response-shaping never leaks internal fields it explicitly reshapes to avoid exposing: `enrollBiometric`'s response strips the hashed challenge token and `failedAttempts` from the returned `config` object; `verifyBiometric`'s response strips the `User` entity's `passwordHash` from the returned `user` object; `getBiometricConfig` reshapes each entry the same way. Also confirmed each endpoint correctly forwards `req.user`/path params to the service (e.g. `disableBiometric`/`deleteBiometricConfig` scope to `req.user.id`, not a client-supplied user id, which would otherwise be a real IDOR risk) and that `checkBiometricAvailable` reports the real enrolled-device count and the 3 supported biometric types.

**Verification:** full auth module suite **6/6 suites, 43/43 tests** (was 4/4, 18/18 before this cycle — the 2 new spec files, run alongside the existing `auth.service.spec.ts`/`auth.controller.spec.ts` etc.); full backend suite **248/248 suites, 1971/1971 tests** (was 246/246, 1946/1946 — +2 suites, +25 tests, zero regressions elsewhere, confirmed via an isolated auth-module run first); full e2e tier unchanged **9/9 real suites, 202/202 real tests**; backend `tsc` (full scope) 0 errors; backend `eslint` (full `src` tree) 0 errors, including both new spec files after an `eslint --fix` pass cleaned up pure line-wrap/quote-style formatting. A concurrent parallel session continued its own activity on `ocr-intake.service.ts`+spec and (still) `asset-access.service.ts` throughout — confirmed zero file overlap before staging (this cycle only added the 2 new files).

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** `strictNullChecks` still 0 (unchanged), backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, main jest 248/248 (1971/1971, +2 suites/+25 tests), e2e 9/9 (202/202) unchanged. Diff: 2 new files (`biometric.service.spec.ts`, `biometric.controller.spec.ts`). Roadmap item #17: 3 of 9 closed (`PineconeService`, `BiometricService`, `BiometricController`), **6 remain** (`DeviceTokenService`, `PersonalizationService`, `AssetAccessService`, `CacheService`, `SentinelController`, `ERPulseHandoverService`) — `AssetAccessService` is the natural next pick once the concurrent session's edits there settle; otherwise `CacheService` or `SentinelController` are unblocked alternatives.

One correctness-focused test: `getOrSet`'s cache-hit check is `cached !== null`, not a truthiness check — verified a cached `0` is correctly treated as a hit and the factory is never called, which would silently break if that check were ever "simplified" to `if (cached)`.

### Cycle 180 (2026-07-24) — closed the top Reception Desk finding: no server-side duplicate-patient check on the live create path

Three parallel research agents plus direct code verification audited Reception Desk's real state against the two interrupting mega-briefs (full detail in project memory, not duplicated here). The single most patient-safety-relevant finding: `SmartIntakeService.createFromIntake()`/`createVerticalSlice()` — backing `POST /emergency/patients`, `/emergency/intake`, and `/emergency/intake/vertical-slice`, the endpoints the primary NestJS/TypeORM patient store actually uses by default — ran zero duplicate-patient check. A real fuzzy-match engine (`mpi.service.ts`) exists, but only inside the Mongoose intake path, which is off by default (`ENABLE_MONGOOSE_EMERGENCY_OS` unset).

**Before implementing, re-verified the finding against the actual frontend code rather than trusting the backend-only audit at face value** — this caught a real overstatement. The reception UI already has a genuine, working, blocking client-side duplicate gate in three separate places: `ReceptionWorkspace.tsx`'s `createAndRoute()` (opens a confirm modal and returns early on a ≥85% match before ever calling create), `SmartIntake.tsx`'s identity-verification step (`onCreatePatient` only fires after staff explicitly chooses "Create patient" over "Link patient" having reviewed match candidates), and `QuickIntake.tsx`'s `duplicateAcknowledged` checkbox gate. So the real gap was narrower than "no duplicate detection at all" — it was "no *server-side* enforcement," meaning only a caller that bypasses the UI entirely (a script, a different client, a future integration) was actually at risk. Fixed the correct, narrower problem rather than the initially-reported one.

**Implementation.** New `patient-duplicate-detection.ts` — pure functions only (`scorePatientDuplicate`, `findPatientDuplicateCandidates`), deliberately with zero import back into `emergency-os.services.ts` (an earlier draft that included an `@Injectable` wrapper class created a real module-load cycle that would have broken Nest's constructor-param DI metadata — caught before it shipped, not after). Same field weights and score bands as the two existing implementations (frontend `patientDuplicateDetection.ts`, backend `mpi.service.ts`) for consistency, minus phone/email/healthCard — `EmergencyPatient`/the `Patient` TypeORM entity don't carry those fields today.

Wired into `SmartIntakeService` via a new `guardAgainstUnconfirmedDuplicate()`: a ≥85% match with no `confirmDuplicateOverride: true` on the request throws `ConflictException` (409) with the candidate list in the response body, and logs a new `patient_duplicate_flagged` workflow-audit entry either way (blocked or overridden) — so even a caller that ignores the 409 leaves a durable audit trail. Anything below high-confidence is logged but non-blocking, matching the existing 4-band system (`link_after_staff_confirmation` / `possible_duplicate_review` / `manual_review` / `create_new_patient`).

**Preserving the golden path.** Since the reception UI's own client-side gate already resolves the human decision before its API call fires, threaded `confirmDuplicateOverride: true` through every known trusted internal caller so none of them regress: `receptionIntakeOrchestrator.ts`'s `syncReceptionPatientToBackend` (covers `ReceptionWorkspace`'s `createAndRoute`/`handleProvisionalUnknown` paths), `QuickIntake.tsx`'s submit handler, `SmartIntake.tsx`'s vertical-slice fallback path, and `emergencyStore.ts`'s `addPatient({syncToBackend: true})` branch (currently unreachable in practice — no live caller passes `syncToBackend: true` today — but fixed for whenever it is). Found one dead code path along the way (`receptionQuickIntakeService.ts`'s `persistReceptionQuickIntakePatient` is only referenced by its own test, not by any live component) — left untouched, out of scope, noted for a future cleanup pass.

**Tests.** `patient-duplicate-detection.spec.ts` (11 tests): exact score-band boundaries verified precisely rather than loosely (e.g. a matching MRN alone lands at *exactly* 35, the manual-review floor, not just "somewhere above a threshold"), case/whitespace insensitivity, conflicting-field score penalties, the `limit`/`minScore` options. `smart-intake.service.spec.ts` (7 tests): the 409-block path, the override-and-log path, the non-blocking low-confidence path, and `createVerticalSlice` never reaching `movePatientToState` when blocked.

**Verification.** Full backend suite **251/251 suites, 2018/2018 tests** (was 249/249, 2000/2000 — +2 suites, +18 tests, zero regressions); backend `tsc` both configs 0 errors; backend `eslint` full-tree 0 errors (1 genuine unused-import warning caught and fixed — `EmergencyPatient` in `emergency-os.controller.ts`, orphaned after switching its DTO type). Frontend `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors; frontend `eslint` clean on all touched files. Frontend Vitest run could not execute — same pre-existing `esbuild`/Windows Application Control sandbox block documented since Cycle 160, unrelated to this change; checked every test file that references the touched API functions (`receptionIntakeOrchestrator.test.ts`, `emergencyOsApi.test.ts`, `receptionQuickIntakeService.test.ts`, `R12EndToEndWiring.test.tsx`) by hand and confirmed none assert exact call-argument counts that the new optional second parameter would break. Checked `git status` before staging — a concurrent parallel session's unrelated diff (`ocr-intake.service.ts`+spec, `asset-access.service.ts`) confirmed untouched and excluded.

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, main jest 251/251 (2018/2018, +2 suites/+18 tests), frontend `tsc` 0 errors. Diff: 3 new backend files (`patient-duplicate-detection.ts`, `patient-duplicate-detection.spec.ts`, `smart-intake.service.spec.ts`), 3 backend files edited (`emergency-os.services.ts`, `emergency-os.controller.ts`, `emergency-os.types.ts`), 5 frontend files edited (`emergencyOsApi.ts`, `receptionIntakeOrchestrator.ts`, `QuickIntake.tsx`, `SmartIntake.tsx`, `emergencyStore.ts`). Roadmap item #18 opened and its duplicate-check sub-item closed same-cycle; validation and fire-and-forget-write sub-items remain open for a future cycle.

### Cycle 181 (2026-07-24) — closed `DeviceTokenService` on roadmap item #17; corrected a self-introduced overstatement from Cycle 180

User said "let's do the next cycle." Re-checked `git status` first, as always: `AssetAccessService` still carries the identical small uncommitted diff from the concurrent parallel session — unchanged since Cycle 179, still off-limits. With roadmap item #18's duplicate-check sub-item closed last cycle, considered continuing straight into item #18's remaining "near-zero body validation" sub-item, since the context was still warm. Looked closely enough to size it first rather than assume it's a quick follow-on: `main.ts`'s global `ValidationPipe` already runs with `whitelist: true, forbidNonWhitelisted: true, transform: true` — meaning the moment `SmartIntakeCreateInput` becomes a real class-validator DTO instead of a plain interface, **any field the frontend sends that isn't explicitly declared on the DTO gets rejected with a 400**, not silently stripped. `EmergencyPatient`'s full shape includes several nested arrays/objects (`vitals[]`, `notes[]`, `timeline[]`, the `arrival` block, `triageAssist`, `quickSafetyFlags`, `highRiskComplaintFlags`) that would all need their own nested DTOs to whitelist correctly, and at least one real caller (the "unknown patient" chooser flow) deliberately sends minimal data expecting the service to backfill defaults — a DTO that's too strict would break that flow outright. Concluded this needs its own dedicated, carefully-scoped pass (enumerate every field every real caller sends today before turning on enforcement), not a same-cycle continuation — left it open on the roadmap rather than rush it.

Also re-examined item #18's third original sub-item, "fire-and-forget DB write" (`persistPatientToDatabase()` not awaited), before deciding whether it was a fast win instead. Reading the method's own doc comment settled it: *"Phase 1 of the DB migration: best-effort, non-blocking write-through... Reads still come from the in-memory array... Never throws; a failed write here must not break the synchronous in-memory response."* This is a deliberate, already-documented design choice, not an overlooked bug — and it's the exact mechanism already tracked as open roadmap item #2 ("Cut `EmergencyPatientService` reads to the database (Phase 2)... needs a dedicated design pass"). Awaiting the write wouldn't even improve correctness today, since nothing reads from the DB yet — it would just add latency to every mutation for no behavioral benefit. Corrected item #18 to remove this sub-item rather than let last cycle's overstatement stand; the underlying concern lives correctly under item #2.

With both #18 sub-items now correctly re-scoped as "needs its own pass, not this cycle," picked the next roadmap item #17 candidate instead: `DeviceTokenService` (`backend/src/modules/notifications/services/device-token.service.ts`, 209 lines) — push-notification device-token registration and lifecycle management, flagged since Cycle 167 and the longer-standing of the 2 remaining unblocked candidates (`PersonalizationService`, flagged Cycle 168, is the other). Read the full file before writing anything: real register-or-update-in-place branching, a soft-delete (`deactivateToken`) vs. hard-delete (`removeDeviceToken`) distinction with an OR-matching lookup (token or device id), a multi-user token-grouping aggregation (`getTokensForUsers`), and a `QueryBuilder`-based bulk cleanup (`cleanupOldTokens`) with date-cutoff arithmetic.

**Wrote 16 tests** (`device-token.service.spec.ts`), following the established `Test.createTestingModule` + `getRepositoryToken` mocked-repository pattern (matching `biometric.service.spec.ts`'s style) rather than inventing a new one:
- **`registerDeviceToken`**: confirms a genuinely new token calls `create()` with the right shape; confirms re-registering an existing token updates the row in place (`create()` not called) and correctly flips `isActive` back to `true` even if it had been previously deactivated; confirms a repository failure propagates rather than getting silently swallowed (the method's own `try/catch` re-throws after logging, so this asserts that re-throw actually happens, not just that logging occurred).
- **`getUserDeviceTokens`/`getActiveTokens`**: verifies the exact `where`/`order` query shape (active-only, newest-used-first) and that `getActiveTokens` correctly projects down to just the token strings.
- **`deactivateToken`/`removeDeviceToken`**: both `NotFoundException` guard paths; confirmed `deactivateToken` never calls `delete()` (soft-delete only) and `removeDeviceToken` never calls `save()` (hard-delete only) — these are easy to accidentally conflate since both "remove a device" conceptually; confirmed `removeDeviceToken`'s OR-array `where` clause is constructed exactly as the source builds it (token-match OR id-match), not just that some matching call happened.
- **`markTokenAsInvalid`/`updateLastUsed`**: both are direct `update()` calls with no read first — confirmed via `expect(repo.findOne).not.toHaveBeenCalled()` on the invalid-token path, since an unnecessary read-before-write here would be a real (if minor) inefficiency worth catching.
- **`getTokensForUsers`**: the one method with real aggregation logic — built a fixture with 2 users, one having 2 devices, and confirmed the resulting `Map` correctly accumulates both of that user's tokens into one array rather than the second device overwriting the first (`tokenMap.get(userId)!.push(...)` is correct, but a `tokenMap.set(userId, [...])` typo one line up would silently break this and every test would still "pass" on the single-device case).
- **`cleanupOldTokens`**: mocked the full `createQueryBuilder().delete().where().andWhere().execute()` chain; under `jest.useFakeTimers().setSystemTime(...)`, verified the computed cutoff date to the **exact ISO timestamp** (not just "before now") for both the 90-day default and an explicit 30-day override — cross-checked the expected values with a standalone `node -e` date computation in the same environment before writing the assertion, rather than trusting mental arithmetic on month-boundary date rollover; also verified the `result.affected ?? 0`-shaped fallback returns `0` rather than `null`/`undefined` when the driver reports no count.

**Verification.** New suite **1/1, 16/16**. A first full-suite run showed **1 failing test** unrelated to this change: `encryption.service.spec.ts`'s `should hash values quickly` performance assertion (1000 hashes expected `<100ms`, measured `343.93ms`) — this ran concurrently with several other `tsc`/`eslint` commands in the same session, a plausible source of CPU contention. Rather than wave it off, isolated the one spec file (`37/37` clean) and then re-ran the entire suite alone with nothing else contending for CPU (**252/252 suites, 2034/2034 tests**, fully clean) — confirming it was a transient load-sensitivity flake in a hard-coded wall-clock threshold, not a regression from this cycle's change (a device-token test file has no relationship to the encryption module's hashing throughput). Backend `tsc` (both configs) 0 errors; backend `eslint` 0 errors on both the new spec file and the untouched source file. No frontend files touched this cycle. `git status` re-checked before staging — the concurrent session's diff (`ocr-intake.service.ts`+spec, `asset-access.service.ts`) confirmed still untouched and excluded.

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, main jest 252/252 (2034/2034, +1 suite/+16 tests). Diff: 1 new file (`device-token.service.spec.ts`), plus this cycle's own `SCORECARD.md` roadmap correction (no source changes for the correction itself). Roadmap item #17: 5 of 9 closed, **4 remain** (`PersonalizationService`, `AssetAccessService` [still blocked], `SentinelController` [still deferred, size], `ERPulseHandoverService`). Roadmap item #18: duplicate-check sub-item stays closed (Cycle 180); fire-and-forget-write sub-item removed from scope (folded into item #2); validation sub-item remains open, now explicitly scoped as "needs its own dedicated pass," not a quick follow-on.

**Lesson: correcting your own prior cycle's roadmap entry, in public, in the same file, is exactly the kind of "verify before trusting — including self-generated docs" discipline this whole program has applied to the codebase's own audit artifacts (the Reception Health Report contradiction from the cycle before this thread even started) — it applies just as much to this file's own claims from one cycle ago. A doc comment inside the code itself (`persistPatientToDatabase`'s "Phase 1 of the DB migration") was the actual ground truth the whole time; the fix was re-reading it carefully during scoping, not writing new code. Also: a global `ValidationPipe` configured with `forbidNonWhitelisted: true` is a meaningfully different, higher-stakes thing to newly activate on a route than it sounds — until a route's body type becomes a real decorated class, that global config is silently inert for that route specifically, and turning it on is a behavior change with real regression surface (a payload shape that's tolerated today can start getting hard-rejected), not just "finally enabling validation that already runs everywhere else."**

### Cycle 182 (2026-07-24) — first full dependency/security re-audit in 22 cycles

User said "let's do one more scan," which was ambiguous enough (accessibility? circular deps? unused exports? dependencies?) to ask rather than guess — a genuine fork where picking wrong could burn a full cycle on the wrong target. User picked the dependency/security audit. Last real check was Cycle 160 (2026-07-23) — 22 cycles and a lot of new code ago, so treated this as a fresh baseline rather than a quick "still fine?" glance.

**Frontend (`npm audit`):** 3 vulnerabilities — `postcss` (high, path-traversal in sourcemap auto-loading, `GHSA-r28c-9q8g-f849`, transitive via `vite`), `react-router`/`react-router-dom` (2 moderate CVEs each). Ran `npm audit fix --dry-run` first rather than apply blind: it proposed bumping `postcss` 8.5.15→8.5.23 and `nanoid` 3.3.12→3.3.16 (both safe transitive patch bumps under `vite`), plus **removing `esbuild-wasm@0.28.1`** — investigated that before trusting it, since an unexplained package removal is exactly the kind of side effect worth checking rather than accepting on faith. Found `esbuild-wasm` physically present in `node_modules` but with **zero references anywhere in `package-lock.json`** — a stray, untracked package (plausibly a leftover from a past attempt to work around this session's long-documented `esbuild` Application Control sandbox block, since `esbuild-wasm` doesn't need a native binary spawn — but not actually wired into anything). Its removal is a safe reconciliation, not a functional change. Applied `npm audit fix` (non-force): postcss's high-severity finding closed cleanly.

`react-router`/`react-router-dom` needed real investigation before deciding whether to fix or defer: checked the npm registry directly and confirmed **6.30.4 (currently installed) is the latest 6.x release** — there is no patched 6.x version. Both CVEs (`GHSA-wrjc-x8rr-h8h6` open-redirect-via-backslash, `GHSA-337j-9hxr-rhxg` SSR-hydration constructor injection) are only fixed in the 7.x line (latest 7.18.1). React Router 7 merged Remix's data-loader/route-definition patterns — a real migration across this app's ~190+ routes, not a version-bump drive-by, and the SSR-hydration CVE likely doesn't even apply here (this app is a Vite SPA, not SSR). Deliberately left unfixed and opened as new roadmap item #19, rather than either silently skip it or attempt a rushed major-version migration mid-audit-cycle.

**Backend (`npm audit`):** 5 vulnerabilities — the already-tracked `@xenova/transformers`/`sharp` cluster (roadmap item #5, high), plus 2 new ones: `mongoose` (moderate, prototype pollution via `__proto__`-prefixed dotted paths in update casting, `GHSA-664h-wqgq-64gw`) and `tar`/`sqlite3` (moderate, uncontrolled-recursion DoS via crafted long-path tar archives, `GHSA-r292-9mhp-454m`). Same dry-run-first discipline: `mongoose` 9.7.0→9.8.0 and `tar` 7.5.19→7.5.22 were both offered as safe non-force fixes, though `mongoose`'s bump pulls in a newer `mongodb` driver (7.2.0→7.5.0) with its own sub-dependency churn (removed `bson`/`whatwg-url`/`tr46`/`mongodb-connection-string-url`, added updated equivalents) — a real dependency-tree change worth verifying, not assuming equivalent. Applied `npm audit fix` (non-force): both moderate findings closed.

Re-checked `@xenova/transformers`/`sharp` (item #5) with fresh evidence rather than carrying forward an assumed-still-true note: `npm audit fix --force --dry-run` still only offers downgrading `@xenova/transformers` to **1.4.2** — older than the currently-installed 2.17.2, not a real fix, matching the exact conclusion already on record since Cycle 160. Left untouched, roadmap entry updated to note the re-confirmation with a date rather than just leaving last cycle's note to imply it was never re-checked.

**Verification, deliberately thorough given this touches the Mongoose/MongoDB stack directly** (the off-by-default Express intake path documented in the Reception Desk audit, plus whatever else depends on `mongoose`): frontend `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors (only `package-lock.json` changed, no `package.json` version-range edits, so no source-level ripple expected — confirmed anyway). Backend `tsc` both configs 0 errors; full `eslint` sweep (whole `src` tree, not just changed files, matching the Cycle 176 lesson about per-file linting missing drift) 0 errors; **full jest suite 252/252 suites, 2034/2034 tests**, unchanged from before the dependency bump; **full e2e tier 9/9 real suites, 202/202 real tests**, unchanged, run specifically because this cycle touches the Mongoose/MongoDB dependency chain directly and the e2e tier is the closest thing this program has to an integration check on that stack. `git status` confirmed only `package-lock.json` (both trees) changed — no source files touched, nothing to conflict with the concurrent parallel session's ongoing `ocr-intake.service.ts`/`asset-access.service.ts` edits.

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** frontend `tsc` 0 errors, frontend `npm audit` 2 moderate (was 3: 2 moderate + 1 high) — 1 high closed, 2 moderate newly found and correctly deferred (not previously on record). Backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, backend `npm audit` 2 high (was 5: 3 moderate + 2 high) — both moderate closed, the 2 high (`@xenova/transformers`/`sharp`, one finding counted twice) re-confirmed unchanged with no safe fix. Main jest 252/252 (2034/2034) unchanged, e2e 9/9 (202/202) unchanged. Diff: 2 lockfiles only (`package-lock.json`, `backend/package-lock.json`), `SCORECARD.md`. Roadmap: item #5 re-confirmed with a fresh date; new item #19 opened for `react-router`/`react-router-dom`.

**Lesson: a "quick dependency audit" is only actually quick for the fixes that are genuinely safe — the discipline that made this cycle useful was running `--dry-run` before every `npm audit fix` and reading the exact diff (not just the vulnerability count) before applying it, twice catching something worth a second look before trusting it: a package the dry-run said it would silently remove (turned out to be safe — untracked stray weight, not a real dependency), and a "fix available: true" flag that turned out to require `--force` and a downgrade once actually attempted (turned out to not be a real fix at all, for react-router). `npm audit`'s summary-line vulnerability counts and its `fixAvailable` boolean are both worth treating as a starting hypothesis to verify, not a number to react to directly — the same "verify before trusting" discipline this program applies to code applies just as much to tooling output.**

### Cycle 183 (2026-07-24) — closed `AssetAccessService`, the roadmap item #17 candidate blocked longest by a concurrent session

Between cycles, the user separately asked to "commit all changes and push" — the working tree at that point held only 3 modified files, all belonging to the concurrent parallel session this program has been tracking since Cycle 174 (`ocr-intake.service.ts`+spec, `asset-access.service.ts`) plus 2 untracked `.tmp-*` scratch directories. Read the actual diffs before committing rather than trust the "concurrent session, leave alone" label uncritically after so many cycles of it sitting unchanged: all 3 turned out to be pure prettier line-wrap formatting, zero semantic change, and had been sitting stable and uncommitted for at least 9 cycles (Cy174 through Cy182) without ever actually being touched further — evidence this wasn't really an actively "concurrent" session by that point, just old uncommitted formatting nobody had swept up. Verified safe (`tsc`/`eslint` clean, all 16 relevant suites/60 tests passing) and committed them; deliberately left the 2 `.tmp-*` directories out (compiled `.js` scratch copies of `src/` files from some earlier ad-hoc test harness, not real project state) and flagged that exclusion to the user rather than silently deciding either way.

That commit had a direct effect on this cycle's target selection: with `asset-access.service.ts` now clean in git status, `AssetAccessService` — the single strongest evidence-backed candidate on the roadmap item #17 list since Cycle 171 found a real bug there (`recommendedAssetIds` vs. the entity's actual `preferredAssetIds`, silently zeroing every user's role-based asset recommendations) purely for lack of test coverage — was finally unblocked after 4+ cycles of deferral on concurrency grounds alone.

Read the full 272-line file before writing anything. `resolveAccess()` is a genuine access-control decision engine: a priority-ordered cascade of 9 checks (user-hidden, admin-only governance, draft/archived lifecycle, human-review-required, unsupported backend, demo/entitlement gating, workspace restriction, deprecated/beta lifecycle, default-allowed) that determines whether a given user can see/use a given platform asset — security/entitlement-relevant code in the same category this program has prioritized before (Biometric, Cache, DeviceToken). It's also unusually test-friendly: `resolveAccess` is public and takes fully explicit params, so the entire decision engine could be tested directly without mocking any of the 3 injected collaborator services at all.

**Wrote 39 tests** (`asset-access.service.spec.ts`), split into the pure decision-engine (no mocks) and the orchestration layer (3 mocked collaborators):

- **The full cascade, in priority order, including precedence checks, not just isolated branches**: confirmed `user-hidden` wins over an admin-only asset even for a student (the very first check short-circuits everything downstream); confirmed all 4 independent signal sources that trigger the admin-only gate (`governance.adminOnly`, `governance.visibility === 'admin-only'`, `governance.audience === 'admin-only'`, `permissionPolicy.adminOnly`) each work on their own; confirmed an admin passing the admin-only gate doesn't short-circuit straight to `ALLOWED` — it pushes an `'admin-lifecycle-override'` reason and keeps evaluating every check below it (verified by pairing it with a `BETA` lifecycle asset and confirming both reasons appear, in order); `DRAFT`/`ARCHIVED` lifecycle hides an asset from everyone including an admin; a `requiresHumanReview` asset blocks a student but not other roles.
- **The demo/entitlement sub-branch, the most conditionally nested part of the method**: a demo-labeled asset is `DEMO_ONLY` with no org entitlement gating in effect, but flips to `LOCKED` the moment the org has any entitlement packs configured and this specific asset isn't in them — and back to `DEMO_ONLY` once it is; for non-demo assets, confirmed the subtle "open-access org" distinction — `hasOrganization: true` with an *empty* entitled set is `ALLOWED` (no gating configured yet) unless `strictEntitlements` is also set, in which case an empty entitled set locks everything. This distinction is easy to get backwards when reading the code casually, so tested it explicitly rather than assume it from the variable names.
- **Priority ordering and its one real invariant**: pinned beats workspace-recommended beats role-recommended beats recent (tested by setting all four simultaneously and confirming only the highest-priority reason wins at each step down), and — the one genuinely important safety property — a `LOCKED` asset always gets priority 900/`'permitted'` regardless of whether it's pinned, since `resolvePriority`'s very first check short-circuits on any non-`ALLOWED`/non-`DEMO_ONLY` state. Without this test, a future refactor that moved the pinned-check earlier could silently let a user "pin" their way past an entitlement lock in the UI's sort order.
- **The full `effectiveAccessState` mapping table** (the internal `AssetAccessState` → external `SaasAssetAccessState` translation), via a parametrized `it.each`, plus 3 extra cases for the `ALLOWED` branch specifically: unpinned/unrecommended maps to `'visible'`, pinned maps to `'pinned'`, role-recommended maps to `'recommended'` — and, deliberately tested because it's easy to assume otherwise, a merely-*recent* asset still maps to `'visible'`, not `'recommended'`, since `toSaasAccessState` only checks the `pinned`/`*-recommended` priority reasons, not `recent`.
- **Orchestration** (`getUserAssetAccess`, 3 collaborators mocked): the default asset-id list is the union of entitled ids and every known asset when none are explicitly requested, vs. scoped to exactly the requested ids when they are; sorting is priority-first (a pinned asset out of alphabetical order still sorts first) with a genuine alphabetical tiebreaker case for equal-priority assets (not just asserted by test title — actually exercised with two unpinned assets); legacy workspace tool-id alias resolution verified against a real alias that expands to a *different* literal asset id (`medical-iot` → `['telemetry-monitoring', 'medical-iot']`), not just a self-mapping; pinned/hidden/recent ids correctly merge across all 3 legacy preference-field shapes (`pinnedAssetIds`/`pinnedToolIds`/`saasProfile.pinnedAssets`) rather than only reading the newest one.

**Verification:** new suite **1/1, 39/39**; backend `tsc` (both configs) 0 errors; backend `eslint` 0 errors (6 pure prettier line-wrap findings in the new spec file, fixed via `eslint --fix`, re-verified all 39 tests still pass after); full backend suite **253/253 suites, 2073/2073 tests** (was 252/252, 2034/2034 — +1 suite, +39 tests, zero regressions). No frontend files touched. `git status` confirmed clean of any concurrent-session state before staging — this cycle's own commit from the prior turn had already cleared it.

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, main jest 253/253 (2073/2073, +1 suite/+39 tests). Diff: 1 new file (`asset-access.service.spec.ts`). Roadmap item #17: 6 of 9 closed, **3 remain** (`PersonalizationService`, `SentinelController` [still deferred, size], `ERPulseHandoverService`).

**Lesson: the "check `git status` before picking a target" discipline paid off in a new way this cycle — not by steering away from a blocked file (the usual case, Cy178/179/181), but by the blocker actually clearing between cycles as a side effect of an unrelated user request ("commit all changes"), which is exactly why re-checking fresh every cycle matters more than trusting last cycle's "still blocked" note. Also: when a file has looked "concurrently in flux" for many cycles running with an unchanged diff, it's worth actually reading that diff at some point rather than perpetually deferring on the same assumption — this one turned out to be inert, pre-existing formatting debt, not live work in progress, and the label had outlived its accuracy by several cycles.**

### Cycle 184 (2026-07-24) — closed `PersonalizationService`, the last unblocked roadmap item #17 candidate before `SentinelController`/`ERPulseHandoverService`

User said "let's go the next cycle." `git status` clean of any concurrent-session state (the last cycle's commit had already cleared it). Picked `PersonalizationService` (`backend/src/modules/personalization/personalization.service.ts`, 145 lines) — flagged since Cycle 168, the only remaining roadmap item #17 candidate not already deferred for size (`SentinelController`) or already partially covered (`ERPulseHandoverService`). Read the full file first: AI-preference storage (preferred behavior, suggested tools, recommended workflows, recent-prompt history) plus a full saved-prompt CRUD surface, backed by 2 TypeORM repositories.

**Wrote 16 tests** (`personalization.service.spec.ts`), following the established `Test.createTestingModule` + `getRepositoryToken` mocked-repository pattern:

- **`getForUser`'s defaulting logic, tested at both points it can trigger, not just the obvious one**: the first-ever call for a user creates a real default row (`preferredBehavior: 'clinical_copilot'`, a real 3-tool `suggestedTools` default, the literal `DEFAULT_RECOMMENDATIONS` array) — but also, separately, confirmed the *same* defaults apply when an existing row is found with `suggestedTools`/`recommendedWorkflows`/`recentPrompts` explicitly `null` (a genuinely different code path — `preference.suggestedTools || [...]` firing on a null value from an existing row, not the create-time default assignment). Easy to test only the create path and assume the `||` fallback is redundant; it isn't, since a real DB row could plausibly have these columns null from a schema migration or a partial write.
- **`getForUser`'s saved-prompts serialization**: confirmed the exact `find()` query shape (`userId` scope, `updatedAt DESC`, `take: 25`) and — a real security-relevant check — that `serializePrompt()`'s output genuinely has no `userId` field, not just an `undefined` one, since the raw entity passed into it does carry `userId` and a careless refactor (e.g. an object spread instead of an explicit field list) could leak it into an API response.
- **`updateForUser`'s partial-update semantics**: confirmed a dto with only `preferredBehavior` set leaves `suggestedTools`/`recommendedWorkflows` completely untouched (the `if (dto.x)` guards are per-field, not a merge-everything-or-nothing), and confirmed `suggestedTools` genuinely deduplicates via `[...new Set(...)]` rather than storing verbatim duplicates.
- **`recommendationsForUser`'s allow-list filtering**: the empty-list-means-unrestricted case (default param `[]`), the actual filtering case (both `suggestedTools` and workflow entries filtered down to the allowed set), and — the one non-obvious rule worth its own test — a workflow recommendation with no `toolId` at all always survives filtering regardless of the allow-list, since the filter condition is `!workflow.toolId || allowed.size===0 || allowed.has(...)` and a missing `toolId` short-circuits the whole check to true.
- **`savePrompt`/`deletePrompt`**: title trimming, `tags` defaulting to `[]`, `workspaceId` genuinely omitted (not stored as an empty string) when not given; `deletePrompt`'s `NotFoundException` guard and — the actually security-relevant assertion — that both the existence check and the delete itself are scoped to `{id, userId}` together, not just `{id}`, confirming one user can't delete another user's saved prompt by guessing an id.
- **`recordRecentPrompt`**: whitespace-collapsing + 160-character truncation, and the 10-entry cap (built a fixture with exactly 10 existing entries, recorded an 11th, and confirmed the oldest was evicted while the newest landed at index 0 — not just "length is still 10").

**Caught a wrong assumption in my own test before it ever ran, not after a red result**: the whitespace-truncation test's first draft asserted `promptPreview.startsWith('multi space text')` against an input that began with leading whitespace. Before trusting that assertion, ran the exact `.replace(/\s+/g, ' ')` logic standalone in Node against the literal test input — it revealed the collapsing regex leaves a single leading space rather than stripping it (`"  multi..."` → `" multi..."`, not `"multi..."`), which would have made the `startsWith` check fail against a string that started with a space. Fixed the test's input (removed the leading whitespace) rather than weaken the assertion — same "verify before trusting, including your own not-yet-run test code" discipline this program has applied to test authoring since Cycle 177's Pinecone metadata mistake, just caught pre-emptively this time instead of via a failing run.

**Verification:** new suite **1/1, 16/16**; backend `tsc` (both configs) 0 errors; backend `eslint` 0 errors (2 pure prettier findings in the new spec file, fixed via `eslint --fix`, re-verified all 16 tests still pass after); full backend suite **254/254 suites, 2089/2089 tests** (was 253/253, 2073/2073 — +1 suite, +16 tests, zero regressions). No frontend files touched. `git status` re-checked clean before staging.

**Final state, all fresh this cycle:** backend `tsc`/`eslint` both 0 errors, main jest 254/254 (2089/2089, +1 suite/+16 tests). Diff: 1 new file (`personalization.service.spec.ts`). Roadmap item #17: 7 of 9 closed, **2 remain** (`SentinelController` [still deferred, size], `ERPulseHandoverService`).

**Lesson: verifying a test's own logic against a quick standalone script before trusting it — not just before trusting the code under test — catches a real class of self-authored bug that a first "run it and see" pass would also have caught, but catching it *before* the first run means the eventual green result is actually informative (confirms the fixed assertion is correct) rather than ambiguous (a passing test whose author never independently checked whether the assertion itself matched reality is only as trustworthy as the author's mental model, which is exactly what this incident showed can be wrong even about a one-line regex).**
